What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Two weeks ago I was in a severe car wreck, and ever since then, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my tax work or make simple decisions—it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife urged me to come in because I'm falling so far behind that my business is suddenly at risk.\n\nDoctor: Since the car wreck, have you been experiencing any intense fear, anxiety, or avoidance of things that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm constantly on edge and terrified, jumping at every little noise like a slamming car door and always scanning for danger. It's this incredibly intense anxiety that just won't let up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling of being detached from your body when you're reminded of the accident, like when you're near that intersection or riding in a car?\n\nPatient: When it happens, everything around me suddenly feels completely fake and distant, like I'm watching a movie of my own life instead of living it. I feel like I'm not actually inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. It's incredibly unsettling and makes me feel totally disconnected from what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this feeling of being detached from your body or surroundings at times when you're not reminded of the car accident, such as during daily routines or when you're relaxed?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't really happen when I'm relaxed or just sitting at home. It mostly strikes me during daily routines that involve triggers, like when I have to ride in a car or walk past the intersection where the crash happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any other situations or triggers, aside from reminders of the car accident, that might cause you to feel this intense anxiety or detachment from your body?\n\nPatient: The out-of-body detachment really only happens when I'm in a car or near that intersection. However, I get hit with intense physical anxiety and startle reactions from everyday things, like my wife quietly walking into my office or a car door slamming outside. Those sudden, everyday noises make my heart race and my hands shake, even though they aren't direct reminders of the accident.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant and persistent feelings of guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the car accident, or has your main distress been focused on the anxiety and detachment symptoms you've described?"

All of my distress is really about the constant anxiety, being so jumpy and on edge, and that strange, flat feeling of numbness and detachment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of detachment from your body or surroundings since the car accident, is it happening almost every day or just occasionally?"

It really feels like I'm just watching someone else's life instead of living my own."

I feel like I'm not actually inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. It's incredibly unsettling and makes me feel totally disconnected from what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this feeling of being detached from your body or surroundings at times when you're not reminded of the car accident, such as during daily routines or when you're relaxed?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't really happen when I'm relaxed or just sitting at home. It mostly strikes me during daily routines that involve triggers, like when I have to ride in a car or walk past the intersection where the crash happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any other situations or triggers, aside from reminders of the car accident, that might cause you to feel this intense anxiety or detachment from your body?\n\nPatient: The out-of-body detachment really only happens when I'm in a car or near that intersection. Those sudden, everyday noises make my heart race and my hands shake, even though they aren't direct reminders of the accident.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant and persistent feelings of guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the car accident, or has your main distress been focused on the anxiety and detachment symptoms you've described?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any guilt or self-blame about the crash, since the other driver ran a red light and hit me. All of my distress is really about the constant anxiety, being so jumpy and on edge, and that strange, flat feeling of numbness and detachment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of detachment from your body or surroundings since the car accident, is it happening almost every day or just occasionally?\n\nPatient: It doesn't happen constantly all day, but it has been occurring regularly over the last two weeks whenever I have to ride in a car or pass near that intersection. During those specific moments, I feel like I'm watching a movie of my own life or floating a few feet behind myself. Even when my wife shares good news or my son sends me a funny video, I just feel flat and unable to feel happy or grateful. It really feels like I'm just watching someone else's life instead of living my own.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulty recalling specific details about the car accident itself, or does your memory of the event seem fairly clear to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, there is actually a major gap in my memory. I can vividly recall seeing the headlights coming at me, the truck spinning, and the sound of metal crunching, but there is a complete blank spot between that and suddenly finding myself outside on a stretcher. It really feels like I'm just watching someone else's life instead of living my own.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulty recalling specific details about the car accident itself, or does your memory of the event seem fairly clear to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, there is actually a major gap in my memory. I can vividly recall seeing the headlights coming at me, the truck spinning, and the sound of metal crunching, but there is a complete blank spot between that and suddenly finding myself outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I just can't remember what happened during those minutes.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you have experienced any other dissociative symptoms aside from feeling detached from your body, such as feeling like you're in a dream or that things around you aren't real, outside of the contexts of car rides or being near the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do experience those feelings of things seeming fake or far away, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. While it is definitely strongest and most easily triggered when I'm in a car or near the intersection, it can also happen at other times, making me feel completely disconnected and floating outside my own body."

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Two weeks ago I was in a severe car wreck, and ever since then, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my tax work or make simple decisions—it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife urged me to come in because I'm falling so far behind that my business is suddenly at risk.\n\nDoctor: Since the car wreck, have you been experiencing any intense fear, anxiety, or avoidance of things that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm constantly on edge and terrified, jumping at every little noise like a slamming car door and always scanning for danger. When I'm forced to ride in a car or go near that intersection, everything suddenly feels weirdly fake and far away, like I'm detached from my own body. It's this incredibly intense anxiety that just won't let up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling of being detached from your body when you're reminded of the accident, like when you're near that intersection or riding in a car?\n\nPatient: When it happens, everything around me suddenly feels completely fake and distant, like I'm watching a movie of my own life instead of living it. I feel like I'm not actually inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. It's incredibly unsettling and makes me feel totally disconnected from what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this feeling of being detached from your body or surroundings at times when you're not reminded of the car accident, such as during daily routines or when you're relaxed?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't really happen when I'm relaxed or just sitting at home. It mostly strikes me during daily routines that involve triggers, like when I have to ride in a car or walk past the intersection where the crash happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any other situations or triggers, aside from reminders of the car accident, that might cause you to feel this intense anxiety or detachment from your body?\n\nPatient: The out-of-body detachment really only happens when I'm in a car or near that intersection. However, I get hit with intense physical anxiety and startle reactions from everyday things, like my wife quietly walking into my office or a car door slamming outside. Those sudden, everyday noises make my heart race and my hands shake, even though they aren't direct reminders of the accident.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant and persistent feelings of guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the car accident, or has your main distress been focused on the anxiety and detachment symptoms you've described?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any guilt or self-blame about the crash, since the other driver ran a red light and hit me. All of my distress is really about the constant anxiety, being so jumpy and on edge, and that strange, flat feeling of numbness and detachment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of detachment from your body or surroundings since the car accident, is it happening almost every day or just occasionally?\n\nPatient: It doesn't happen constantly all day, but it has been occurring regularly over the last two weeks whenever I have to ride in a car or pass near that intersection. During those specific moments, I feel like I'm watching a movie of my own life or floating a few feet behind myself. So it happens quite frequently, basically anytime I face those situations.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of detachment from your body or surroundings, and the world feeling fake, interfering with your ability to enjoy activities or be present in social interactions outside of the specific triggers like cars or the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, that feeling of being detached definitely carries over into my social life and interactions. Even when my wife shares good news or my son sends me a funny video, I just feel flat and unable to feel happy or grateful. It really feels like I'm just watching someone else's life instead of living my own.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulty recalling specific details about the car accident itself, or does your memory of the event seem fairly clear to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, there is actually a major gap in my memory. I can vividly recall seeing the headlights coming at me, the truck spinning, and the sound of metal crunching, but there is a complete blank spot between that and suddenly finding myself outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I just can't remember what happened during those minutes.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you have experienced any other dissociative symptoms aside from feeling detached from your body, such as feeling like you're in a dream or that things around you aren't real, outside of the contexts of car rides or being near the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do experience those feelings of things seeming fake or far away, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. While it is definitely strongest and most easily triggered when I'm in a car or near the intersection, it can also happen at other times, making me feel completely disconnected and floating outside my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if these dissociative feelings, such as feeling like you're in a dream or detached from your body, are primarily triggered by reminders of the car accident or if they also occur spontaneously without any obvious trigger?"

While it is definitely strongest and most easily triggered when I'm in a car or near the intersection, it can also happen at other times, making me feel completely disconnected and floating outside my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if these dissociative feelings, such as feeling like you're in a dream or detached from your body, are primarily triggered by reminders of the car accident or if they also occur spontaneously without any obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are strongly triggered by reminders of the accident. It specifically happens when I am riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash occurred, making everything feel fake and far away. In those moments, it's like I'm watching a movie of my own life from a few feet behind my body."

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Two weeks ago I was in a severe car wreck, and ever since then, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my tax work or make simple decisions—it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife urged me to come in because I'm falling so far behind that my business is suddenly at risk.\n\nDoctor: Since the car wreck, have you been experiencing any intense fear, anxiety, or avoidance of things that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm constantly on edge and terrified, jumping at every little noise like a slamming car door and always scanning for danger. When I'm forced to ride in a car or go near that intersection, everything suddenly feels weirdly fake and far away, like I'm detached from my own body. It's this incredibly intense anxiety that just won't let up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling of being detached from your body when you're reminded of the accident, like when you're near that intersection or riding in a car?\n\nPatient: When it happens, everything around me suddenly feels completely fake and distant, like I'm watching a movie of my own life instead of living it. I feel like I'm not actually inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. It's incredibly unsettling and makes me feel totally disconnected from what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this feeling of being detached from your body or surroundings at times when you're not reminded of the car accident, such as during daily routines or when you're relaxed?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't really happen when I'm relaxed or just sitting at home. It mostly strikes me during daily routines that involve triggers, like when I have to ride in a car or walk past the intersection where the crash happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any other situations or triggers, aside from reminders of the car accident, that might cause you to feel this intense anxiety or detachment from your body?\n\nPatient: The out-of-body detachment really only happens when I'm in a car or near that intersection. However, I get hit with intense physical anxiety and startle reactions from everyday things, like my wife quietly walking into my office or a car door slamming outside. Those sudden, everyday noises make my heart race and my hands shake, even though they aren't direct reminders of the accident.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant and persistent feelings of guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the car accident, or has your main distress been focused on the anxiety and detachment symptoms you've described?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any guilt or self-blame about the crash, since the other driver ran a red light and hit me. All of my distress is really about the constant anxiety, being so jumpy and on edge, and that strange, flat feeling of numbness and detachment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of detachment from your body or surroundings since the car accident, is it happening almost every day or just occasionally?\n\nPatient: It doesn't happen constantly all day, but it has been occurring regularly over the last two weeks whenever I have to ride in a car or pass near that intersection. During those specific moments, I feel like I'm watching a movie of my own life or floating a few feet behind myself. So it happens quite frequently, basically anytime I face those situations.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of detachment from your body or surroundings, and the world feeling fake, interfering with your ability to enjoy activities or be present in social interactions outside of the specific triggers like cars or the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, that feeling of being detached definitely carries over into my social life and interactions. Even when my wife shares good news or my son sends me a funny video, I just feel flat and unable to feel happy or grateful. It really feels like I'm just watching someone else's life instead of living my own.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulty recalling specific details about the car accident itself, or does your memory of the event seem fairly clear to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, there is actually a major gap in my memory. I can vividly recall seeing the headlights coming at me, the truck spinning, and the sound of metal crunching, but there is a complete blank spot between that and suddenly finding myself outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I just can't remember what happened during those minutes.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you have experienced any other dissociative symptoms aside from feeling detached from your body, such as feeling like you're in a dream or that things around you aren't real, outside of the contexts of car rides or being near the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do experience those feelings of things seeming fake or far away, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. While it is definitely strongest and most easily triggered when I'm in a car or near the intersection, it can also happen at other times, making me feel completely disconnected and floating outside my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if these dissociative feelings, such as feeling like you're in a dream or detached from your body, are primarily triggered by reminders of the car accident or if they also occur spontaneously without any obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are strongly triggered by reminders of the accident. It specifically happens when I am riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash occurred, making everything feel fake and far away. In those moments, it's like I'm watching a movie of my own life from a few feet behind my body.\n\nDoctor: Are there any times when you feel completely normal and connected to your body and surroundings, without any of the detachment or anxiety symptoms, or are these feelings of detachment and anxiety always present to some degree?"
  }

The anxiety and feeling on edge have been pretty much constant ever since the crash. However, the feelings of detachment aren't always there; they only happen in specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where the accident occurred.

It specifically happens when I am riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash occurred, making everything feel fake and far away. In those moments, it's like I'm watching a movie of my own life from a few feet behind my body.\n\nDoctor: Are there any times when you feel completely normal and connected to your body and surroundings, without any of the detachment or anxiety symptoms, or are these feelings of detachment and anxiety always present to some degree?\n\nPatient: The anxiety and feeling on edge have been pretty much constant ever since the crash. However, the feelings of detachment aren't always there; they only happen in specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where the accident occurred."
  }

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Two weeks ago I was in a severe car wreck, and ever since then, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my tax work or make simple decisions—it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife urged me to come in because I'm falling so far behind that my business is suddenly at risk.\n\nDoctor: Since the car wreck, have you been experiencing any intense fear, anxiety, or avoidance of things that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm constantly on edge and terrified, jumping at every little noise like a slamming car door and always scanning for danger. When I'm forced to ride in a car or go near that intersection, everything suddenly feels weirdly fake and far away, like I'm detached from my own body. It's this incredibly intense anxiety that just won't let up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling of being detached from your body when you're reminded of the accident, like when you're near that intersection or riding in a car?\n\nPatient: When it happens, everything around me suddenly feels completely fake and distant, like I'm watching a movie of my own life instead of living it. I feel like I'm not actually inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. It's incredibly unsettling and makes me feel totally disconnected from what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this feeling of being detached from your body or surroundings at times when you're not reminded of the car accident, such as during daily routines or when you're relaxed?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't really happen when I'm relaxed or just sitting at home. It mostly strikes me during daily routines that involve triggers, like when I have to ride in a car or walk past the intersection where the crash happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any other situations or triggers, aside from reminders of the car accident, that might cause you to feel this intense anxiety or detachment from your body?\n\nPatient: The out-of-body detachment really only happens when I'm in a car or near that intersection. However, I get hit with intense physical anxiety and startle reactions from everyday things, like my wife quietly walking into my office or a car door slamming outside. Those sudden, everyday noises make my heart race and my hands shake, even though they aren't direct reminders of the accident.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant and persistent feelings of guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the car accident, or has your main distress been focused on the anxiety and detachment symptoms you've described?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any guilt or self-blame about the crash, since the other driver ran a red light and hit me. All of my distress is really about the constant anxiety, being so jumpy and on edge, and that strange, flat feeling of numbness and detachment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of detachment from your body or surroundings since the car accident, is it happening almost every day or just occasionally?\n\nPatient: It doesn't happen constantly all day, but it has been occurring regularly over the last two weeks whenever I have to ride in a car or pass near that intersection. During those specific moments, I feel like I'm watching a movie of my own life or floating a few feet behind myself. So it happens quite frequently, basically anytime I face those situations.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of detachment from your body or surroundings, and the world feeling fake, interfering with your ability to enjoy activities or be present in social interactions outside of the specific triggers like cars or the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, that feeling of being detached definitely carries over into my social life and interactions. Even when my wife shares good news or my son sends me a funny video, I just feel flat and unable to feel happy or grateful. It really feels like I'm just watching someone else's life instead of living my own.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulty recalling specific details about the car accident itself, or does your memory of the event seem fairly clear to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, there is actually a major gap in my memory. I can vividly recall seeing the headlights coming at me, the truck spinning, and the sound of metal crunching, but there is a complete blank spot between that and suddenly finding myself outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I just can't remember what happened during those minutes.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you have experienced any other dissociative symptoms aside from feeling detached from your body, such as feeling like you're in a dream or that things around you aren't real, outside of the contexts of car rides or being near the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do experience those feelings of things seeming fake or far away, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. While it is definitely strongest and most easily triggered when I'm in a car or near the intersection, it can also happen at other times, making me feel completely disconnected and floating outside my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if these dissociative feelings, such as feeling like you're in a dream or detached from your body, are primarily triggered by reminders of the car accident or if they also occur spontaneously without any obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are strongly triggered by reminders of the accident. It specifically happens when I am riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash occurred, making everything feel fake and far away. In those moments, it's like I'm watching a movie of my own life from a few feet behind my body.\n\nDoctor: Are there any times when you feel completely normal and connected to your body and surroundings, without any of the detachment or anxiety symptoms, or are these feelings of detachment and anxiety always present to some degree?\n\nPatient: The anxiety and feeling on edge have been pretty much constant ever since the crash. However, the feelings of detachment aren't always there; they only happen in specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where the accident occurred.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience intense anxiety or fear without the accompanying feeling of detachment from your body, versus how often the detachment occurs without intense anxiety?"

No matter how hard I try, I just can't remember what happened during those minutes.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you have experienced any other dissociative symptoms aside from feeling detached from your body, such as feeling like you're in a dream or that things around you aren't real, outside of the contexts of car rides or being near the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do experience those feelings of things seeming fake or far away, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. While it is definitely strongest and most easily triggered when I'm in a car or near the intersection, it can also happen at other times, making me feel completely disconnected and floating outside my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if these dissociative feelings, such as feeling like you're in a dream or detached from your body, are primarily triggered by reminders of the car accident or if they also occur spontaneously without any obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are strongly triggered by reminders of the accident. It specifically happens when I am riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash occurred, making everything feel fake and far away. In those moments, it's like I'm watching a movie of my own life from a few feet behind my body.\n\nDoctor: Are there any times when you feel completely normal and connected to your body and surroundings, without any of the detachment or anxiety symptoms, or are these feelings of detachment and anxiety always present to some degree?\n\nPatient: The anxiety and feeling on edge have been pretty much constant ever since the crash. However, the feelings of detachment aren't always there; they only happen in specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where the accident occurred.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience intense anxiety or fear without the accompanying feeling of detachment from your body, versus how often the detachment occurs without intense anxiety?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's really hard for me to separate the two or keep track of how often one happens without the other. Everything has felt so incredibly overwhelming and jumbled together since the accident that I can't really distinguish between them."
  }

I feel like I'm not actually inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. It's incredibly unsettling and makes me feel totally disconnected from what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this feeling of being detached from your body or surroundings at times when you're not reminded of the car accident, such as during daily routines or when you're relaxed?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't really happen when I'm relaxed or just sitting at home. However, I get hit with intense physical anxiety and startle reactions from everyday things, like my wife quietly walking into my office or a car door slamming outside. Those sudden, everyday noises make my heart race and my hands shake, even though they aren't direct reminders of the accident.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant and persistent feelings of guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the car accident, or has your main distress been focused on the anxiety and detachment symptoms you've described?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any guilt or self-blame about the crash, since the other driver ran a red light and hit me. During those specific moments, I feel like I'm watching a movie of my own life or floating a few feet behind myself. So it happens quite frequently, basically anytime I face those situations.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of detachment from your body or surroundings, and the world feeling fake, interfering with your ability to enjoy activities or be present in social interactions outside of the specific triggers like cars or the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, that feeling of being detached definitely carries over into my social life and interactions. It's this incredibly intense anxiety that just won't let up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling of being detached from your body when you're reminded of the accident, like when you're near that intersection or riding in a car?\n\nPatient: When it happens, everything around me suddenly feels completely fake and distant, like I'm watching a movie of my own life instead of living it. I feel like I'm not actually inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself. It's incredibly unsettling and makes me feel totally disconnected from what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this feeling of being detached from your body or surroundings at times when you're not reminded of the car accident, such as during daily routines or when you're relaxed?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't really happen when I'm relaxed or just sitting at home. It mostly strikes me during daily routines that involve triggers, like when I have to ride in a car or walk past the intersection where the crash happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any other situations or triggers, aside from reminders of the car accident, that might cause you to feel this intense anxiety or detachment from your body?\n\nPatient: The out-of-body detachment really only happens when I'm in a car or near that intersection. However, I get hit with intense physical anxiety and startle reactions from everyday things, like my wife quietly walking into my office or a car door slamming outside. Those sudden, everyday noises make my heart race and my hands shake, even though they aren't direct reminders of the accident.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant and persistent feelings of guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the car accident, or has your main distress been focused on the anxiety and detachment symptoms you've described?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any guilt or self-blame about the crash, since the other driver ran a red light and hit me. All of my distress is really about the constant anxiety, being so jumpy and on edge, and that strange, flat feeling of numbness and detachment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of detachment from your body or surroundings since the car accident, is it happening almost every day or just occasionally?\n\nPatient: It doesn't happen constantly all day, but it has been occurring regularly over the last two weeks whenever I have to ride in a car or pass near that intersection. During those specific moments, I feel like I'm watching a movie of my own life or floating a few feet behind myself. So it happens quite frequently, basically anytime I face those situations.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of detachment from your body or surroundings, and the world feeling fake, interfering with your ability to enjoy activities or be present in social interactions outside of the specific triggers like cars or the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, that feeling of being detached definitely carries over into my social life and interactions. Even when my wife shares good news or my son sends me a funny video, I just feel flat and unable to feel happy or grateful. It really feels like I'm just watching someone else's life instead of living my own.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulty recalling specific details about the car accident itself, or does your memory of the event seem fairly clear to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, there is actually a major gap in my memory. I can vividly recall seeing the headlights coming at me, the truck spinning, and the sound of metal crunching, but there is a complete blank spot between that and suddenly finding myself outside on a stretcher. No matter how hard I try, I just can't remember what happened during those minutes.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you have experienced any other dissociative symptoms aside from feeling detached from your body, such as feeling like you're in a dream or that things around you aren't real, outside of the contexts of car rides or being near the accident intersection?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do experience those feelings of things seeming fake or far away, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. While it is definitely strongest and most easily triggered when I'm in a car or near the intersection, it can also happen at other times, making me feel completely disconnected and floating outside my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if these dissociative feelings, such as feeling like you're in a dream or detached from your body, are primarily triggered by reminders of the car accident or if they also occur spontaneously without any obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: Yes, they are strongly triggered by reminders of the accident. It specifically happens when I am riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash occurred, making everything feel fake and far away. In those moments, it's like I'm watching a movie of my own life from a few feet behind my body.\n\nDoctor: Are there any times when you feel completely normal and connected to your body and surroundings, without any of the detachment or anxiety symptoms, or are these feelings of detachment and anxiety always present to some degree?\n\nPatient: The anxiety and feeling on edge have been pretty much constant ever since the crash. However, the feelings of detachment aren't always there; they only happen in specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or passing the intersection where the accident occurred.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience intense anxiety or fear without the accompanying feeling of detachment from your body, versus how often the detachment occurs without intense anxiety?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's really hard for me to separate the two or keep track of how often one happens without the other. Everything has felt so incredibly overwhelming and jumbled together since the accident that I can't really distinguish between them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if the feeling of being detached from your body or the world feeling fake is more distressing to you, or is the constant anxiety and fear of potential dangers more bothersome in your daily life?\n\nPatient: While the feeling of detachment and the world feeling fake is deeply unsettling when it happens, especially in cars, the constant anxiety is much more bothersome on a daily basis. Being constantly on edge and scanning for danger is just relentless and exhausting. It completely prevents me from relaxing, even when I'm in my own home."
